30,2026-10-01,"The number of people applying for U.S. unemployment benefits dipped last week to the lowest level since mid-July as layoffs remain low and and most American workers enjoy job security
WASHINGTON -- The number of people applying for U.S. unemployment benefits dipped last week to the lowest level since mid-July as layoffs remain low and most American workers enjoy job security.
The Labor Department said Thursday that initial jobless claims ticked down to 197,000 from a revised 198,000 the week before. The four-week average of claims, which smooths out week-to-week volatility, dropped by 2,500 to 200,000.
Claims for jobless benefits are a proxy for layoffs, and economists watch them because they can be a sign of where the job market is headed. So far this year, claims have mostly stayed below 220,000 — historically low.
The American job market has remained sturdy despite higher energy prices that have squeezed businesses and consumers since the fighting with Iran began Feb. 28.
The U.S. on Tuesday reported that U.S. employers posted fewer job openings in August. The Labor Department’s Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey (JOLTS) also showed that layoffs fell and the number of people quitting their jobs — a sign of confidence in their prospects — retreated slightly in August. The report’s measure of gross hiring — before subtracting people who quit or lost their jobs — ticked up modestly in August but remains at low levels. 
“Jobless claims continue to defy expectations and remain exceedingly low, dovetailing with the JOLTS reports’ layoff rate which shows that while businesses aren’t hiring at a rapid pace, they are reticent to let their current crop of workers go,” wrote Matthew Martin, senior U.S. economist at Oxford Economics. 
Layoffs are low. Businesses, remembering the labor shortages that followed the end of pandemic lockdowns, are reluctant to let go of staff. They’re hiring — but modestly by the standards of recent years.
So far this year, employers — companies, government agencies and nonprofits — have been adding an average of 80,000 jobs a month, including a surprising 162,000 in August. That is an improvement on a lackluster 2025 when monthly job creation averaged 9,700 as high interest rates and President Donald Trump’s unpredictable trade policies discouraged hiring.
The Labor Department will release the jobs report for September next week. It’s expected to show that employers added 90,000 jobs and that the unemployment rate remained low at 4.1%, according to a survey of forecasters by the data firm FactSet.
Hiring remains well below the 166,000 monthly jobs created, on average, in 2023 and 2024, and the 491,000 a month recorded during the 2021-2022 hiring boom that followed COVID-19 lockdowns.

Reviewed:


                        The Rulers: Corporate Power in the Age of AI and the Cloud 


                        Silicon Empires: The Fight for the Future of AI 
One of the hallmarks of the modern corporation is the separation of ownership from control. The firm is owned by shareholders but led by managers, who answer to shareholders only indirectly. Actually, it’s weirder than that. The corporation owns itself. Shareholders merely own stock, which is a claim on the firm’s assets and earnings.
Elements of this arrangement have been around for centuries. Scholars have detected traces of early “joint-stock” companies in tenth-century China. In 1602 the Dutch East India Company conducted the first initial public offering in history, floating its shares on the newly created Amsterdam stock exchange. But the publicly traded corporation achieved a new prominence in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, during the period of upheaval and dynamism known as the second industrial revolution.
Observing the situation in Germany—the only other country that was industrializing as rapidly as the United States at the time—a young Viennese economist named Rudolf Hilferding saw a new stage of capitalism emerging. He called it “finance capital” in an influential book of that title published in 1910. At the heart of Hilferding’s analysis was a simple observation. Building the railroads, stockyards, and steel mills of the new economy required large sums of money. These sums could not be sourced from companies’ existing revenue. They had to be obtained from external parties, by printing pieces of paper that told stories about the future: bonds that promised a stream of interest payments and stocks that promised a rising share price and a portion of the profits distributed as dividends.
Capital markets in London, New York, and Berlin arose to facilitate the buying and selling of these pieces of paper. In doing so, they effectively pooled wealth from across the world and put it at the service of industry. “The obstacles which arise from the fragmentation of capital among a host of indifferent and casual owners are removed,” wrote Hilferding. “The corporation can draw directly upon the combined capital of the capitalist class.”
Thus unfettered, an enterprise could grow larger. In Hilferding’s view, finance capital initiated a new era of monopoly, spawning giant trusts and cartels that did not compete in the traditional sense. Rather, they acted as market planners, coordinating investment and production across the global economy. By holding controlling stakes in networks of subsidiaries, men like J.P. Morgan and John D. Rockefeller could personally administer entire sectors. The separation of ownership from control had, paradoxically, created new ways to recombine the two in more concentrated form.
More than a hundred years later, we are living through another era of monopoly. Finance is still with us—and even more integral to the economy than it was in Hilferding’s day—but the seat of oligarchic power is American tech. Five companies in California and Washington have a combined market capitalization of about $20 trillion. Earlier this year Elon Musk became humanity’s first trillionaire by taking SpaceX—a company whose monstrous valuation rests in large part on the promise of putting AI data centers in space—public. A trillion is a number that, like space, operates at a scale that strains human understanding. One is reminded of the narrator in Thomas Mann’s Doctor Faustus, who denounces the chilly vastness of the cosmos as “Luciferian.”
Remarkably, such figures actually understate the importance of the tech sector. As the law professor Frank Pasquale has argued, the top firms and their leaders are not only very rich but “functional sovereigns,” choreographing our lives with a thoroughness that would be the envy of the tsars.
 The Rulers: Corporate Power in the Age of AI and the Cloud is the most comprehensive picture yet of how our new sovereigns acquire and exercise power. Written by the Argentine economist Cecilia Rikap, the book doesn’t begin with technology, or even with the tech sector. Instead, Rikap’s starting point is the “intellectual monopoly.” Intellectual monopolies are “organizations that systematically capture rents derived from their sustained concentration of intangible assets,” she writes. In her view, Coca-Cola is an intellectual monopoly. So are Disney, Pfizer, and Nike.
An intangible asset, according to the definition that accountants learn in school, is an asset that lacks a physical form: patents, trademarks, copyrights, trade secrets. These are the sorts of intangible assets that are protected by intellectual property (IP) law, and they are the basis for some very profitable lines of business. Coca-Cola has its secret recipe; Disney has Donald Duck. But “IP only covers part of a much more extensive monopolization of knowledge, data and narratives,” notes Rikap. She is far more interested in how companies collect and analyze information about their suppliers, franchises, customers, and employees. This might involve tracking the flow of shipping containers around the world or the movement of workers around a warehouse. It might involve scrutinizing a consumer’s purchase history or a shop’s sales history. Data can be gleaned from any source, and hammered with software until it yields a useful insight.
Advertisement
Rikap traces the origins of intellectual monopolization in the broadest sense to the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries—Hilferding’s era. It was then, she argues, that several kinds of intangible assets first became essential to the world economy. One of these was scientific knowledge. Historically, the scientist was typically a gentleman ensconced in a private home laboratory. But as advances in chemistry and physics yielded valuable new methods and machinery, industrialists began putting scientists on their payroll and building R&D labs, while “polytechnic” schools on the German model churned out engineers.
The emergence of a mass consumer base, meanwhile, compelled companies to differentiate their products by investing in another kind of intangible asset: branding. Among the pioneers was Coca-Cola, which designed a distinctive glass bottle for its beverages, blanketed cities with colorful billboards, and gave away branded clocks, calendars, and wallets. Brand management became a major concern in the postwar decades, as what Allen Ginsberg called the “nitroglycerine shrieks of the fairies of advertising”—he worked for five years on Madison Avenue, unhappily—began their long assault on our senses.
In these years the United States was mostly in the business of making stuff. You needed the Don Drapers to sell what rolled off the assembly line, and the scientists to invent transistors and nylons. But these “knowledge workers,” as they would later come to be called, still served an economy centered on manufacturing, which didn’t peak as a percentage of total employment until 1953 and whose workforce kept growing in absolute terms until 1979.
It was in that last decade that the relationship between ideas and industry decisively changed. The American economy had been slowly deindustrializing since the end of World War II, but the process accelerated in the 1970s, as American companies started outsourcing the manufacture of physical goods to overseas suppliers in much larger numbers. While these suppliers were separate companies, in practice they served at the pleasure of the outsourcing firm, whose power lay in the possession of certain lucrative intangible assets. Rikap cites the example of Nike. The Swoosh, the designs, and the attendant IP are the real sources of value. The shoes themselves are made by contractors in countries such as China and Vietnam.
Nike tells its suppliers what to make and how much it’s willing to pay. It is a market planner, in other words, comparable to the financiers that fascinated Hilferding. But finance capital exerted control through ownership: Rockefeller called the shots because he owned pieces of many different companies. Intellectual monopolization, by contrast, is distinguished by what Rikap describes as “control beyond ownership.” A company like Nike still engages in more conventional forms of monopolistic behavior, like buying up competitors. But the innovation of intellectual monopoly capitalism is that capitalists don’t always need to incur the costs of ownership in order to enjoy its benefits. They can boss people around without taking responsibility for them.
Computers made it possible to implement this kind of dominance at a distance. When industry went overseas, it also changed form, evolving from the large, vertically integrated enterprises associated with Henry Ford into disaggregated ensembles of mines, mills, workshops, and sheds woven together in global supply chains. Monitoring and managing this complex distributed system would be impossible without computers. Supply chains are, as the scholar Miriam Posner argues in a forthcoming book, artifacts of software.1 Among the legacies of the digital revolution are not only the PC and the Internet but a world where something as simple as a pair of socks is constructed through the combined efforts of contractors in several countries before landing in your local Gap.
The tech sector didn’t just facilitate the rise of intellectual monopoly capitalism, however. It has also produced the most successful intellectual monopolies. Software is an excellent intangible asset. The philosopher George Caffentzis observes that capitalist commodity production always involves “the reproduction of a pattern,” and the patterns of software cost nearly nothing to reproduce. This is the magic money tree that, by 1995, had made Bill Gates the richest man in the world.2
But Rikap identifies another reason for Big Tech’s success: in its most recent iteration, it has discovered a way to outsource the creation of intangible assets. The archetypal case is that of Internet platforms. As everyone (hopefully) knows, companies like Google and Facebook make money from the data that we exude by searching, liking, and posting, which is used to sell targeted advertisements. The systems that supervise this churn are created and maintained by programmers sitting in Silicon Valley, but the material is supplied by us.
Advertisement
For this reason, the returns on platforms are even better than those from traditional software. Gates and his employees had to make their intangible assets themselves. What the platforms have managed to do is take the logic of outsourcing one step further and turn us all into subcontracted (though uncompensated) knowledge workers. This innovation, Rikap suggests, has reached its apotheosis with large language models (LLMs), which can be considered “co-produced by everyone connected to the internet” through the training data we provide with our collective activity.
The platforms’ chief technology for harvesting the Internet’s intellectual value is the cloud. Concretely, the cloud refers to the planetary archipelago of data centers that started to appear in the 2000s, initially as the enabling infrastructure for the platforms. Then in 2006 Amazon began selling computer storage and processing capacity as an on-demand service to other companies. This inaugurated the business of cloud computing, which has grown enormously over the past two decades. (Amazon Web Services posted a profit of $45.6 billion last year.) From the Pentagon to the CIA, hospitals to Hollywood to Wall Street, everyone uses the cloud. Estimates vary, but the evidence suggests that the vast majority of the world’s organizations rely on the technology in some form.
As a result, the “hyperscalers” that own the largest clouds—Amazon, Google, and Microsoft—effectively run the information layer of the global economy. While most intellectual monopolies are “confined to specific sectors,” these firms control “digital production, exchange, and consumption networks” in such a way that enables them “to subordinate organizations across virtually all industries,” writes Rikap, including other intellectual monopolies. The machinery of the cloud is always humming in the background of our economic lives.
Let’s say you want to buy a lawnmower. You do a Google search, and Home Depot pops up. You look at the options and find one you like. The site says it’s in stock at a store a few miles away. You drive over, look at it, ask the guy working there a few questions, and decide to buy it.
Where does the cloud fit into this sequence of events? For one thing, it ran the Web applications that helped you find the lawnmower, from Google to the Home Depot site. And, as you might expect, data was created about you along the way, such that you will be seeing ads for lawnmowers everywhere you look online from now on, perhaps for the rest of your life. But the cloud also coordinated the various corporate systems that made your purchase possible, from the software that ensures lawnmowers are in stock at that particular Home Depot to the program that tells headquarters when it’s time to reorder more lawnmowers from the supplier to the service that manages the payroll and schedules the hours of the employee who helped you.
Here in the single grain of a Sunday afternoon lives a galaxy of computation. Multiply the transaction by a billion or two and you get a sense of how much information passes through the hands of the hyperscalers each day. (Home Depot, incidentally, uses Google Cloud.) Not only do they extract a steady stream of rents from the millions of enterprises that rely on them, they acquire “a vantage point over the global economy by hosting its digital twin,” writes Rikap. This gives them a real-time map of world commerce from which they can “extract economic insights” and “infer business trends.” No capitalist in history has enjoyed such a panoptic view of market civilization.
But maybe a map isn’t quite the right metaphor. The cloud doesn’t just model the economy; the two are, increasingly, co-constitutive. At a certain resolution, a copy becomes indistinguishable from the original. In the early 1980s the philosopher Jean Baudrillard coined the term “hyperreality” to describe this phenomenon. He was thinking mostly of television, but the most hyperreal thing ever invented is the cloud.
The most inspired moments in The Rulers come from Rikap’s unorthodox use of dependency theory, a social-scientific tradition cofounded by another Argentine economist, Raúl Prebisch, that focuses on the problem of global poverty. Its adherents in the decolonizing 1960s and 1970s argued that poor countries were poor because rich countries were rich: the latter composed the exploiting core of the “world system,” the former its exploited periphery.
Rikap’s move is to apply this core-and-periphery schema to tech. Dependency theorists wanted to explain how the West kept extracting value from the Third World even after colonial rule had ended. In other words, they were trying to understand a kind of “control beyond ownership.” In recent years writers have often reached for the language of imperialism and colonialism when describing the rapacious behavior and globe-spanning ambitions of Silicon Valley.3 The implicit argument of The Rulers is that a better analogy is the world system that arose after decolonization, in which the old machinery of empire had to be reengineered for a new era of nominally sovereign states. The contractors linked together by the global supply chains that began to emerge in this period were, like the countries themselves, formally but not functionally independent of their masters in the metropole.
In Rikap’s account, Silicon Valley inherits and intensifies this logic. It organizes multiple peripheries, each populated by entities that are technically separate from the monopolizing core. Through platforms, the cloud, and AI, “Big Tech companies systematically transform knowledge and information produced by each and every individual and organization into their assets,” Rikap writes. “They are, in essence, the world’s largest knowledge predators.”
Tech’s peripheries don’t exist just online, however. They also take less digitized forms. One important periphery for tech is academia, Rikap explains. Industry giants “curate research fields according to their interests” by partnering with universities and collaborating with academic researchers. The knowledge is produced in common, but its value accrues to industry. “Between 2012 and 2021,” Rikap writes, “Microsoft co-authored 88 per cent of its more than 15,000 scientific publications with other organizations but shared ownership of only 1 per cent of its 35,233 patents.”
Then there is the start-up world, where companies like Microsoft, Google, and Amazon are major investors, which gives them “preferential access to capabilities, knowledge, and information” held by smaller firms operating at the cutting edge. Because they tend to move faster and take bigger risks, these firms effectively function as outsourced R&D labs. The monopolies can enjoy access to start-ups’ technology and expertise without shouldering the responsibilities of an acquisition. Tech companies still do, of course, buy some businesses directly. But they mainly interact with start-ups in their capacity as venture capitalists, which offers yet another channel for exerting “control beyond ownership.”
In fact, this “pattern of innovation,” to quote Rikap, is what brought about the current AI boom. In 2019 Microsoft made its first investment in OpenAI. The deal gave OpenAI use of Microsoft’s cloud infrastructure, which was required to train the computationally intensive LLMs that OpenAI’s scientists had begun to piece together. Microsoft, in turn, “effectively steered the company’s research priorities, projects and overall direction—from code to product development,” writes Rikap. When OpenAI struck gold with the release of ChatGPT in 2022, its patron reaped the rewards. The following year, Microsoft’s stock price rose nearly 60 percent, propelled in large part by its status as the exclusive reseller of OpenAI’s technology through the cloud.
Contemporary tech oligarchy also resembles a core-periphery arrangement in the more literal, geographic sense. Most of the planet lives under American tech hegemony; even advanced economies such as Europe and Japan have nothing comparable to Silicon Valley. The great exception is China, which has done vastly more than any other country to resist peripheralization. In recent years this has become a problem for Western capital, albeit one of its own making.
The outsourcing blitz of the 1970s that Rikap associates with the birth of intellectual monopoly capitalism coincided with China’s tentative embrace of private enterprise, initiated by Deng Xiaoping at the end of the decade. Over the years that followed, the Pearl River Delta became an industrial megacity, far larger than Manchester or Detroit at their height, as China took on the tasks that the West was busily offshoring.
But China had the means to aspire to more. It began climbing the value chain, muscling into higher-end sectors and creating intellectual monopolies of its own: national champions in advanced industries, guided by the “technodevelopmentalism” policies of the Communist Party. Sooner or later, Western firms were alarmed to find their contractors becoming their competition.
Meanwhile, US companies doing business in China “found that the benefits of access were less than imagined,” as the political economist Nick Srnicek notes in his new book, Silicon Empires: The Fight for the Future of AI. Foreign firms were often required to form joint ventures with domestic ones, which enabled Chinese companies to gain technical expertise, much to the irritation of the Americans.
The resulting frictions caused some American business leaders to grow wary of China, creating an opening for the hawks within the national security state to reassert themselves. Obama announced his “Pivot to Asia” in 2011, an attempt at diplomatic and military containment; Trump stoked rising tensions into a trade war in 2018; Biden added a raft of export controls after entering the White House in 2021 and started talking about a global struggle between “democracies” and “autocracies.” More recently, Trump has fashioned a kind of détente with Xi Jinping, reducing tariffs and loosening restrictions on the sale of AI chips, although most of the Biden-era export controls remain in place.
Srnicek is one of the most perceptive scholars of our networked age. His Platform Capitalism (2016) is a much-cited taxonomy of the economic arrangements created by Google, Uber, and others. Silicon Empires is a sequel of sorts, applying the same clinical precision to the commotion around generative AI. But what makes the book especially valuable is how it situates the AI boom within a wider set of political and geopolitical developments. These developments could be described as an inflection point in the evolution of intellectual monopoly capitalism—a new stage in Rikap’s story.
Understanding this latest shift requires looking at the relationship between intellectual monopolies and the state. Historically, intellectual monopolies wanted a relatively hands-off government, one that cleared the field for “market forces” by encouraging global economic liberalization. This held true for Silicon Valley until fairly recently. From the 1990s until the mid-2010s, there was a “broad agreement across both the political elite and tech elite,” Srnicek writes, that the Internet would “create an American-led world of borderless commerce and data.” The “Silicon Valley Consensus,” as he calls it, was to seek maximum globalization with minimal regulation. Economic interests were paramount; national security concerns “took a backseat.” And the Chinese market was a top priority. In 2015 Mark Zuckerberg traveled to Beijing and asked Xi to give his unborn daughter an honorary Chinese name. (Xi declined.)
Over the last decade or so, two forces have combined to pull the Silicon Valley Consensus apart. The first is what some commentators call “deglobalization”: countries around the world are drifting away from global economic integration. In the aftermath of the market shocks inflicted by the pandemic, Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine, and the US–Israeli attack on Iran, among other factors, many political leaders have come to see their exposure to world markets as a source of risk, to be mitigated by bringing production back within their borders (“reshoring”) or those of allied countries (“friendshoring”), or pursuing other efforts to shorten and secure supply chains. Matters once regarded as purely economic are now downstream of war.
The second threat to the consensus is the precipitous growth of generative AI. As a technology, generative AI is capital-intensive. It requires a lot of data centers, which in turn require a lot of energy. For this Silicon Valley needs help from the government. The Trump administration has been happy to oblige, opening up federal land for data center construction, scuttling environmental review, and even offering financial support. But the resource requirements of AI are so great that the industry has to venture overseas to meet them. “In a move reminiscent of earlier forms of imperialism, AI companies are pushing abroad to get more data to train their models, more energy to run their data centres, more water to cool their servers, more land to house it all, and more capital to fund their projects,” writes Srnicek. “All of this requires significant support from the state to be achieved.”
Equally important is finding customers. The high cost of AI development means that international expansion is essential for obtaining a decent return on investment. The goal, Srnicek explains, is to lock other countries into the American AI “stack,” such that they become reliant on Nvidia chips, Amazon data centers, Anthropic models, and the rest. This too requires coordination between state and capital: given the financial and infrastructural demands of AI, foreign governments are necessarily involved.
So is the US government, which has an obvious interest in seeing nations increase their dependency on Silicon Valley while, at the same time, restricting the sale of advanced AI models and semiconductors to those it considers security threats. In early 2025 the Biden administration announced its “AI Diffusion Rule,” which sorted the world into three tiers: close allies like European countries would face the fewest hurdles, adversaries like China and Russia would be embargoed, and everyone else would have to apply for a license to import AI technology. Using export controls to hobble China serves the double duty of undermining both a geopolitical rival and an economic competitor, one with a dynamic tech sector that presents a threat to American AI dominance. All this brings us some distance from the globalizing 1990s, 2000s, and early 2010s, when the young Zuckerberg could tell people that Facebook was founded “to make the world more open and connected.”
One hundred and sixteen years ago, Hilferding noticed a similar transition taking place. The new conglomerates and cartels required more robust forms of government intervention than those offered by the minimalist “night-watchman state” of an earlier era (in the famous phrase of the German socialist Ferdinand Lassalle). They needed a state that could protect industry from overseas competition and facilitate “the conquest of foreign markets”—including through literal conquest. In Hilferding’s view, the high prices charged by monopolies reduced domestic demand for their goods, forcing them to expand abroad, and economic expansionism often went hand-in-hand with the territorial sort.4 These were the years when European and Japanese armies were carving up Africa and Asia, sparking a series of inter-imperial scuffles that eventually escalated into World War I. So far, the 2020s have more than a passing resemblance to Hilferding’s 1910s: hostile blocs of state–capital hybrids, partitioning the globe.
The comparison is not comforting. It suggests that we are sleepwalking our way into the next world war. Still, Srnicek offers reasons to hope that history is unlikely to take exactly the same course. Among the things that differentiate our present moment from “previous periods of great power struggle” is “the scale of interdependence that ties together competing states.” The United States and China remain profoundly intertwined despite more than ten years of economic rivalry. It’s not possible to fully decouple them, even if, as Srnicek says, “we are moving into a far more fragmented and multipolar world.” The deep architecture of globalization will probably keep nuclear armageddon at bay.
Another pinprick of hope can be found in the new importance of the state. AI holds out the promise of intellectual monopolization in its highest and final form. The ambition of the AI sector is to absorb all human knowledge and use it to automate all human labor. The financial rewards for doing so would be incalculable. But this dream cannot be pursued without an active government partner, which presents an opening for the left. The industry’s growing reliance on Washington makes it more susceptible to political pressure. If and when Congress and the White House change hands, such leverage might be applied toward progressive ends.
Hilferding, for his part, saw the monopoly capitalism of his day as the antechamber of socialism. Finance capital had already done the hard work of unifying the economy; all the workers had to do was seize it for themselves. One can hear echoes of this line of thinking in Bernie Sanders’s recent proposal for a sovereign wealth fund that holds equity in the largest AI companies. The shares would be obtained through a onetime tax, and could earn dividends large enough to provide all citizens with an annual payment of $1,000.
Reasonable people can disagree about whether we should tie the financial fortunes of every American household even more tightly to those of Silicon Valley. But something of comparable ambition will be needed to reckon with the inegalitarian forces that AI represents. Rikap calls for “governing knowledge production as a commons,” by which she means not only distributing its benefits more widely but also “democratically deciding what knowledge—and what technologies—are produced and how.” The choice isn’t between markets and planning, she reminds us, but between different types of planners: the monopolists sitting in Menlo Park and Mountain View, chasing the next trillion, or the rest of the human race.

35,2026-10-01,"Exports jump 83.5 percent to top $120bn for the first time amid ferocious semiconductor demand.
Save
Share
South Korea’s exports have surged to a monthly record amid explosive demand for chips used to power artificial intelligence.
Exports jumped 83.5 percent year-on-year to $120.9bn in September, eclipsing the previous monthly record of $102.25bn set in June, preliminary customs figures showed on Thursday.
Chip shipments accounted for nearly half of exports, soaring more than 260 percent to $60.3bn, according to data from the Korea Customs Service.
Total exports from January through September hit $814.5bn, exceeding those for all of 2025, when exports reached a record $709.7bn.
During the first nine months of the year, the country recorded a trade surplus of $49.85bn.
“This is a valuable achievement that once again demonstrates the strength of our industries and companies, and is thanks to the sweat and efforts of our entrepreneurs who have competed fiercely in the global market,” said Kim Jeong-kwan, South Korea’s minister for trade and industry, in a statement.
“While we expect to achieve $1 trillion in annual exports, the strengthening of global protectionism and tensions in the Middle East remain major variables affecting our exports,” Kim said.
South Korea’s economy has been running red-hot this year amid a global shortage of memory chips used in AI.
Asia’s fourth-largest economy is home to one of the world’s largest semiconductor industries, led by top memory chip makers SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics.
SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics together control roughly 80 percent of the global market for high-bandwidth memory and 60 percent of the market for dynamic random-access memory, according to Counterpoint Research.
South Korea’s nominal gross domestic product (GDP) growth hit a five-decade high in the April-June quarter, skyrocketing 26.4 percent year-on-year.
South Korea’s Ministry of Finance and Economy has forecast real GDP to grow 3 percent in 2026, which would be the strongest performance since 2017, excluding the outlier years of the COVID-19 pandemic.
The AI boom has also propelled the country’s stock market into the ranks of the best-performing bourses this year, with the benchmark Kospi rising nearly 60 percent.

36,2026-10-01,"Meta classifies its data centres as ‘pilot models’ and claims Nvidia chips as research supplies. Its own filings warn that the IRS could overturn billions in savings.
Meta’s new AI-Optimized data center in El Paso
Meta tells the IRS that its AI data centres are an experiment that could fail, The New York Times reported on Wednesday. That lets Meta claim a research tax credit on the AI chips it buys. The credit cut almost $4bn off its tax bill last year.
Kashmir Hill, Jesse Drucker, Eli Tan and Mike Isaac reported the story for The New York Times. They cited four people with knowledge of Meta’s operations and a review of securities filings. No outlet had reported Meta’s use of the credit for data centres before.
“Our investments in A.I. are accelerating every major part of our core business,” Mark Zuckerberg has told investors.
For tax purposes, Meta classifies its data centres as “pilot models”, according to the Times. The research and experimentation tax credit, created in 1981, gives companies a rebate for supplies used in an experiment. Standard business operations do not qualify. Meta is claiming the credit for the chips it buys from companies including Nvidia.
Meta began claiming the credit for its data centres two years ago, according to the Times. It labels chips bound for AI data centres differently from those sent to standard data centres, two people said. Some in its finance department questioned whether the approach would pass the IRS, one person said.
The credit cut Meta’s taxes by $2bn in 2024 and $3.9bn in 2025, filings show. In 2023, it was $700m. Meta is now the biggest beneficiary of the credit among publicly traded companies, the Times found. Its auditor, EY, signed off on the plan and has since pitched the approach to other companies.
Meta sets money aside for tax positions the IRS could challenge. That reserve rose 45% in two years, to $18.74bn from $12.9bn. The first factor Meta lists is uncertainty over its research tax credits. Apple, Amazon, Alphabet and Microsoft also report research credits above $1bn a year. None has flagged the credit as a risk, according to the Times.
“Meta is claiming billions of dollars in tax benefits that its own accountants are telling investors are at risk of being overturned by the I.R.S.,” said Lisa De Simone, a former EY tax adviser.
Meta declined to say what made its data centres experimental. Andy Stone, a Meta spokesman, said the company had invested $200bn in research and development over five years.
“Like other companies that invest at this scale, we use the tax incentives Congress established decades ago,” Stone said.
James Shannon sponsored the credit as a congressman in 1981. He told the Times it was meant for “people power, knowledge, information”.
“This has gone way, way beyond what anybody could have imagined,” Shannon said.
The Joint Committee on Taxation projected the credit would cost the government $32.1bn in 2025. Meta alone would account for more than a tenth of that. Meta is also fighting the IRS over $355m in savings. It had treated Zuckerberg’s 2013 stock options as a research expense.
This month, Meta released its Muse AI agent, which became the most downloaded app on iPhone and Android. On Tuesday, Meta signed a deal for AI computing capacity in Southeast Asia. The same day, Zuckerberg’s AI safety accord was signed at the White House.

37,2026-10-01,"Zapier writes 30% to 40% of the job into the role of the people who drive its AI work. At its private retreat, its team explained what else keeps AI champions from losing their time.
Philip Lakin, Zapier’s director of AI transformation, at Zapier Outpost Windsor
Find a champion. Protect 20% of their time. Wait for it to spread. One of the people in the room with me had run that play before, in the era of robotic process automation. Their leadership agreed to protect 20% of an AI champion’s time. A month later, the manager had taken it back. There was more work to do.
That story came from an operations leader at Zapier Outpost Windsor, an invite-only retreat the automation company ran at Oakley Court, on the Thames near Windsor, from 8 to 10 September. Zapier covered flights, lodging and meals for the people it invited, me included. The day’s sessions were led by Philip Lakin, Zapier’s director of AI transformation, and Alex Maxwell, who runs Zapier’s AI Leaders Lab.
Lakin did not argue. In that case, he said, leadership had never really aligned behind it.
“Unless you create the time and you show that you actually value it, what you compensate, what you incentivize, and what you create time for, it’s never gonna happen,” Lakin said.
Zapier’s own structure reflects this. People on its AI transformation guild sit in their lines of business. But 30% to 40% of their job is transformation work, and Zapier removes that share of their other duties. The role has three parts: listening to users, building, and turning the mandate into things the team can measure.
Spreading it thin fails too. Telling a whole company that AI is 10% of everyone’s job gives nobody a reason to make time.
“When everyone’s responsible for something, no one’s responsible for it,” Lakin said.
The usual pitch for AI is efficiency: save time, cut cost. The people doing the work read it differently. They either have no time to experiment, or they suspect that mapping their own workflows is the first step to replacing them.
The pitch that works, Lakin told the room, is relief: something that removes the part of the job people hate, or that “makes you look good”. At the end of the day, one attendee said the question they would take home was exactly that. Ask what people hate about their job, not how they could save time.
TNW has reported on both halves of that fear. Meta’s chief technology officer has said AI productivity gains mean more work, not time off. Hyundai workers voted to strike over automation. Younger workers report feeling guilty about using AI at work at all.
Someone asked how to identify the people worth investing in, when most of the organisation already has more work than it can carry.
Grady Sokaras, Zapier’s head of sales, gave two routes. You hire the person outright, or you bring a group through a basic workshop and watch. Zapier runs a 90-minute session that teaches a small group to build its first workflow. You see who it clicks for. Then you train those people and make the work 30% to 40% of their job, or all of it.
The company has published its own account of the same approach, including hackathons and internal champions. The theory behind it is that people who know how to build spot better problems, even when they never build the production version themselves.
“Things do not, especially in operations, things do not internally market themselves,” Lakin said. “They have to be marketed.”
That is how a champion keeps the time. Lakin told the room to go back to the managers who control their workload with the figures.
“Does your manager know that you took this number and made it this number? Why would they ever take time away from you?” Lakin said.
Lakin walked the room through a three-layer diagnostic. Start at the bottom: if people in the lines of business are not pushing ideas up to the AI guild, the problem is enablement and skills.
Then the middle. If they push plenty, but nothing gets access to data or moves across departments, the problem is the cross-functional layer. Ticket triage exists in every line of business, and somebody has to turn that into one pattern instead of ten.
Then look up. If both of those work and nothing changes, either the mandate was too vague, or the level below the chief executive is not translating it into anything measurable. VPs, directors and senior managers control the reward structures, and the mandate rarely reaches their own numbers.
One attendee said their company was “a million years away” from creating the kind of role that sits between operations and IT. Kevin Forestell of Zapier pushed back. Given how quickly things are moving, he said, consider halving the time you think you need, or at least hold it lightly.
The person who raised it had already made the point. Nine months ago, they said, their own role did not exist.
Surface-level adoption keeps widening the divide, and investors are funding the training meant to close it. The AI skills firm Multiverse raised $70m in May. Someone has to be given the time, measured on what they do with it, and believed when they show the results.

38,2026-10-01,"The London firm will back about 30 European pre-seed and seed startups, and plans to lead most rounds.
DIG Ventures partners Rytis Vitkauskas, Melissa Klinger and Ross Mason (left to right).
DIG Ventures, the London-based firm led by MuleSoft founder Ross Mason, has closed its third fund at $120M. Fund III will back around 30 European pre-seed and seed-stage startups that build software infrastructure for AI.
Its institutional backers include Horsley Bridge, Sofina, Granite and an unnamed US university endowment. The founders of Slack, Datadog, Nord Security, Cast AI, Supercell and Dash0 have also put money in.
The fund targets what DIG calls control points, meaning the data, identity, compliance and orchestration layers that other software depends on. DIG plans to lead most of its rounds and has already started investing.
The thinking behind it is a shift in the economics of software. Because AI makes code cheaper and faster to write, DIG expects products to become harder to defend, with lasting value moving to the infrastructure underneath them.
“These are the companies whose advantages compound as the cost of building software falls,” said Ross Mason, founding partner at DIG Ventures.
Mason founded MuleSoft, which Salesforce bought for $6.5bn. He runs DIG with Melissa Klinger, formerly MuleSoft’s UK sales lead, and Rytis Vitkauskas, who founded YPlan and was a partner at Lightspeed Venture Partners.
The previous fund, DIG’s first from institutional investors, backed observability startup Dash0. Dash0 reached a $1bn valuation with a $110m Series B in March.
The same fund also backed Taktile, which builds AI tools for banks and insurers. In June, Taktile raised a $110m Series C led by Goldman Sachs Alternatives.
According to DIG, Fund II ranks in the top 10% of funds worldwide of its age and size, based on Carta data. The firm also says 80% of Fund II companies raised more institutional money within two years of its investment.
In addition, more than 90% of the Fund II companies that had launched a product entered the US market within 12 months, DIG says. That is the gap the firm says it wants to close for European founders.
“The continent is not short of technical talent. But the challenge is turning technical advantage into global distribution,” Mason said.

39,2026-10-01,"At the age of 39, I already had two failed marriages under my belt and could never have expected to enter another engagement.  Life held its own plan for me, bringing the man I was searching for all along when I least expected it.
Roger had never married and never expected to be. We began our relationship straight out of my failing second marriage, and we were both at a loss about what our end goal would be — until we fell in love hard and never looked back.
Preparing for my previous weddings was much simpler.  Both families chipped in financially, which seems to be customary.  Roger's and my wedding would be much more difficult to afford.  Roger's father died 13 years ago, and his mother has Alzheimer's disease.  We knew that we would need to find our own way to make our wedding dreams come true.
The most reasonable solution was to opt for a lengthy engagement, giving us over two years' time to plan and budget for our big day.  Roger is a respiratory care specialist and is currently in massage therapy school.  I am a freelance writer and a pet caretaker.  We are raising Roger's daughter and caring for our dog and two cats.  In today's economy, we are living paycheck to paycheck.
Instead of an engagement party, we opted for a Stock the Bar Party.  In lieu of gifts, attendees will bring bottles of spirits to be served at our wedding reception.  Each bottle will earn them a raffle ticket toward a chance to win a free massage.  This option will allow us some relief on alcohol costs and will also give Roger more practice with his massage skills.
The first item that we secured was the venue.  We were able to find a marina that was cost-effective, flexible, and offered both ceremony and reception space.  The tables and chairs are included in the cost, as well as a kitchen area for the reception.  The venue provides security to assist with parking, so there will be no need for parking attendants.  When we called to put down our deposit, we were pleasantly surprised to receive a 10% discount for booking so early.
We created a free wedding website to help us stay organized.  Here are the details of our events, guest list, and registry.  We opted out of sending Save-the-Dates and are only sending invitations.  I use social media to find ideas for everything from décor to planning, and I have joined wedding budget groups online for cost-effective advice.
Roger and I have a large group of friends, and we are enlisting them to help however they can. We knew a local bar owner who headed ""adult bingo"" on Thursday nights, and we knew he would be the perfect mix of crude humor and soft heart to officiate our wedding.  He said it was on his bucket list to marry a couple and that he was honored to do us the favor.  
We have a friend who is glad to DJ for us for a low cost, and a friend-of-a-friend who offered his photography in exchange for a wedding invite.  One of Roger's classmates will be our MC, ensuring the day runs smoothly and directing guests as needed.  We have had plenty of offers from others to help with planning, decorating, and catering.
Try a
                                  Level Up action pack.
Build a simple hydration routine to boost energy, focus, and daily performance.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Ideally, Roger and I would have had a lighter financial burden as we plan for our coming wedding.  In a perfect world, we would have the help of each of our families.  Instead, we find ourselves navigating the process alone. At our age, we understand the value of what truly matters. 
Life may not have dealt me the hand that I expected, but Roger was always in my cards.

40,2026-10-01,"I've been a chef for over 15 years, and even though professional kitchens aren't for everyone, there are several lessons and tricks I learned in culinary school that can come in handy for anyone.
Here are some of the most helpful tips to take your at-home cooking game to the next level.
If you're tired of your cutting board moving around while you use it, place a damp paper or dish towel underneath it.
Whether your board is wood or plastic, this trick will help keep it in place and create a more stable work surface.
A sharp knife is a safe and effective tool, but dull ones can slip off food and cause all sorts of unintended injuries.
You can buy tools to sharpen your knives at home if you don't want to visit a professional. Just do a bit of research to see what type of sharpener works best for your particular knife.
The more you prep, the more you can sit back and relax when it's time to actually cook. Chop up your ingredients, measure out your spices, and set yourself up for success and save time later.
For example, most restaurants strain scrambled eggs the day before, then pour out individual portions to cook.
You can do something similar if you're having people over for brunch — crack your eggs in a container with a tight-fitting lid, mix them up, and keep them in a very cold fridge until you're ready to cook them.
Plus, ingredients like scallions and greens can be washed, sliced, and wrapped in paper towels or kept in an ice bath until ready to use.
When making pasta, get that large pot of water salted, filled up, and on the stove first thing. A lot of prepping can be done while the water boils.
Make sure to salt each individual component of your dish, not just the finished product. If the parts taste great, the whole will too.
Most restaurants actually thin dishes with water. If they can do it, so can you.
If your sauce is too dry or soup is too thick, you don't need to run to the store for more stock or broth — simply turn on the tap.
Just make sure to add a little bit at a time, since you can always pour in more, but you can't take it away.
If a recipe is telling you to use a specific oil or butter, you may want to listen. Not all fats are created equal.
Butter and olive oil burn at high temperatures, so use avocado or grapeseed oil for sautéing.
If you want to use butter, you can clarify it — or remove the milk solids by melting and pouring off the liquid — to keep the flavor high and the risk of burning low.
Butter is the secret to a rich risotto as the ingredient tones down acidity, adds creaminess, and generally makes things taste really good.
Following the beurre monté technique, add a pat of cold butter to a saucepan with a bit of simmering water and swish it around until the mixture is thick and emulsified.
Then, to make the most of the fat, add it to your risotto at the end. This can upgrade things like veggies or pasta, too.
If your sauce is boring or a bit flat, acid may be just the thing to fix it.
Whether it's lemon juice or vinegar, just a splash of acid can make all the difference in brightening a dish.
For the fluffiest pancakes and the lightest desserts, mix all your ingredients only until they're just combined (unless a recipe states otherwise).
Don't worry about leftover lumps. They'll generally absorb and hydrate into the batter as it cooks, making those light-as-air baked goods a reality.
You shouldn't cook everything in your oven the same way or at the same height.
Roast veggies at high heat on the bottom rack for maximum browning, and finish dishes up top under the broiler for a crispy, melted look.
For baking, you should cook most things in the middle so the heat is evenly distributed from top to bottom.
Pull meat and fish out of the fridge about 30 minutes before cooking to temper them, which can help them cook more evenly.
Also, rest your meat on a paper towel to absorb surface moisture and help achieve an evenly browned finished product.
This story was originally published on April 23, 2022, and most recently updated on September 29, 2026.

41,2026-10-01,"Halloween has always been more of a lifestyle than a holiday for me, much to the chagrin of my husband and family.
Although I worked at Spirit Halloween — the seasonal chain known for taking over empty storefronts — for three years, I've been a longtime customer and have even joked that the store was created just for me.
Here are nine mistakes I saw customers make while shopping for their costumes and seasonal decor at the popular Halloween chain.
One of the biggest mistakes I saw customers make was only visiting once per year. The stores get truckloads of new merchandise delivered weekly, so chances are you'll find something new every time you stop by.
I also recommend asking an associate on the sales floor when the next delivery will be. I was always happy to help customers find the latest products.
When shopping for accessories to complete your look, keep your eyes open and peruse the entire store because hidden gems are everywhere.
The checkout line, in particular, is always full of the best selection of chokers, earrings, and other fun novelty items.
If you only plan to wear a costume once, it's fine to go for the cheap stuff. But investing a little more can extend the costume's wear.
Spirit Halloween offers a line of Signature Collection costumes, which cost more but are made with higher-quality fabrics. I've found these pieces can last through several years of Halloween events or cosplay fun.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
And, in my opinion, investing in great accessories to complete the look is just as important. For example, if you're dressing up as a vampire, fangs can add an extra layer to the costume. However, a cheap pair probably won't last the night.
My personal favorite is the Scarecrow brand of fangs because they come in different sizes for a more custom fit.
Makeup can really elevate a costume's look, but let's face it, most Halloween-store makeup isn't top quality. I recommend protecting both your skin and your look by investing in quality products like primer, setting powder, and heavy-duty setting spray.
If you're using prosthetics, be sure to purchase Spirit Gum adhesive and remover. Without it, removing prosthetics can be difficult. And you definitely don't want to join that next-day Zoom meeting still dressed as Hellboy.
Always test makeup and adhesives for allergic reactions by using a small amount of the product on the inside of your wrist. Wait at least 20 minutes to see how your skin reacts.
Before purchasing any products, read the package carefully and search for any irritants or allergens.
Most people are tired of receiving email and text offers, but I think Spirit Halloween's are worth it. By signing up, you'll get a 20% off coupon to use during your next visit.
You'll also receive exclusive emails throughout the year about new props and displays, and notifications when stores near you are opening or hiring.
When purchasing animatronics, always make sure you read the box before making a purchase.
A lot of the animatronics are made for outdoor, covered use only. That means that they can break down from exposure to weather (especially humidity that can mess with the electronics) if left outdoors unprotected.
Most people don't realize that Spirit Halloween has its own tech support hotline. Before returning any electronics to the store, call the Zombie Tech Squad (their number is displayed on all prop directions).
Most times, they can troubleshoot your issue and save you a trip back to the store, which can be super helpful during the busy Halloween season.
Don't skip out on the clearance sales on November 1 and 2.
A few years ago, my store was still putting out new stock until closing on October 31. All of that merchandise is typically discounted by 25% to 50% the next day.

43,2026-10-01,"We show couriers where to park and enter a building.
truemetrics helps organizations to navigate the world more efficiently. We are solving one of the biggest pain points in last mile delivery: how to get parcels, groceries, and food to the right door faster and more efficiently.
Our technology sits in the smartphone of the courier and understands where they park, walk and enter a building. Our technology is right now mapping locations in Germany, Qatar, the UK, Denmark, Sweden, Poland, the Netherlands and many other countries.
YC-backed, profitable, and past €1.5M ARR - selling to enterprise carriers like GLS, DPD, and PostNord. The core works. Now the job is the take-off from €1M to €10M+: strengthening what's working today (the now) and discovering what's next (the new).
We are a tech-driven, customer-obsessed company with a small but highly skilled team, pushing the boundaries of what's possible in mapping and positioning.
You'll work directly with the two founders. Ingo (CEO), a serial founder with a background in engineering and tech consulting, leads go-to-market and customers. Jan (CTO), with a PhD in control theory and a decade of engineering experience, leads tech and product.
We thrive on autonomy, ownership, and speed—which means we're looking for curious problem-solvers who love tackling hard challenges and making an immediate impact. If you want structure, rigid processes, and a slow corporate environment, this is not the place for you.
This is a founders’ associate role, working directly with the founders - no layer in between. You'll spend most of your time running and improving the engine that brings in new business, with real room to also help us figure out what comes next.
Path 1 - Run and grow the outbound engine. Own our outbound machine day to day: build and improve campaigns, manage the pipeline, support deals from discovery onward, and get involved in live customer POCs. You won't just execute an existing playbook - you'll be expected to question it, propose changes, and make it better as you learn it.
Path 2 - Support what's next. Alongside running outbound, you'll get real exposure to the exploratory side of the business: testing a new market or product angle, improving how we work , and producing content that supports the sales motion.
You won't be handed a finished playbook and told to execute it forever - you'll be expected to learn it fast, question it, and improve it as you go.
Beyond that:
We're open on background - the two must-haves above matter more than any résumé line. This role is a great fit for someone early in their career who wants outsized exposure and responsibility rather than a slow, structured graduate program. People who tend to thrive here:
We're especially interested in applicants who have already worked in a startup and understand the pace, ambiguity, and ownership that come with it. Previous experience as a founders' associate is ideal: you'll already know what it means to switch quickly between execution, analysis, customer work, and open-ended problems while working directly with founders.
If you need a team to help you got things done, a slow pace, if you are uncomfortable with uncertainty, or a job where you can coast without ownership, this isn't it. We move fast, iterate quickly, and expect everyone to contribute with initiative and creativity.
The Way We Navigate Today Is Broken
I bet you know the following: ""You Have Arrived At Your Destination!"" — or so Google Maps thinks. In reality, the way couriers navigate is broken: No parking, hard-to-find entrances, and labyrinth-like apartment and office complexes can turn the last meters of delivery into a time-consuming puzzle. truemetrics provides parking positions and entrance locations to enable faster drop-offs.
Our data is generated by analyzing sensor data from drivers' smartphones, allowing truemetrics to identify precise parking and entrance locations, thereby simplifying the last meters of delivery.
truemetrics was founded in 2021 by Ingo Bögemann, Jan Bischof, and David Wulff, and is currently backed by Y-Combinator and notable business angels from the industry.
During his management of Wolt's operations in three of Germany's four regions, David often faced challenges with inefficiencies in the last 50 meters of delivery. Together, they started truemetrics to overcome these challenges. Ingo contributes 10 years of engineering and entrepreneurial experience, along with a successful exit. Jan Bischof holds a PhD in Control Theory and has developed software and AI solutions for nearly a decade.

Google antitrust ruling highlights the gap between concerns over abuse of monopoly power and what federal law covers.
The judge in a pair of closely watched antitrust lawsuits against Google acknowledged the alleged harms suffered by publishers and then dismissed the amended complaints filed by Chegg and Penske Media. The order in the Penske case says that Google’s motion to dismiss was granted without prejudice and that the order is final and appealable.
The judge’s order acknowledges that publishers and the employees of publishers are suffering and notes that he is not unsympathetic.
He wrote:
“The court does not treat Plaintiffs’ alleged harms lightly. Nor is it unsympathetic to the situation publishers now find themselves in, and the knock-on consequences to journalists, educators, and other online creators whose content Google takes and repurposes without compensation.”
The judge did not find that publishers suffered no harm. The reason for dismissal was that the plaintiffs’ allegations did not meet the requirements of federal antitrust law. The judge noted that they failed to establish their monopolization claims.
The judge rejected the plaintiffs’ five categories of claims:
Reciprocal dealing is when two parties agree to exchange business with each other. This refers to the idea that publishers allow Google to crawl and index their content and, in exchange, Google provides referral traffic.
The judge ruled that the plaintiffs did not plausibly allege an actual agreement in which Google provided search traffic in exchange for publishers supplying content.
That ruling was based on the logic that the plaintiffs did not assert a plausible “agreement” or allege the elements of an agreement or the circumstances under which that agreement arose. Judge Mehta also listed other elements of an agreement that were not alleged, including communications between Google and the plaintiffs showing that an agreement was negotiated or discussed.
Then he explained the key reason why the plaintiffs failed:
“Plaintiffs have pleaded only that they have an “expectation” that Google will send them search traffic if they make their content available for free. But an expectation is not an agreement.”
Tying is when a company uses its power over one product to force customers to take a second, separate product. Penske did not plausibly establish that Google Search and AI Overviews are separate products with separate consumer demand.
The judge also ruled that publishers lacked standing to challenge Google’s alleged monopoly maintenance in the general search services market. The word “standing” in this context means the plaintiffs had not suffered the kind of injury required to bring that antitrust claim.
The judge explained:
“To survive a motion to dismiss, Plaintiffs must plausibly establish that they have antitrust standing as to these claims. As with other plaintiffs appearing before this court, they have not.”
The judge offered additional details for his ruling:
“Plaintiffs claim to be participants in the general search services market with antitrust standing under two theories. First, Plaintiffs fashion themselves as “suppliers” of Search Index Data in the market for general search services and “purchasers” of Search Referral Traffic.
Alternatively, and simultaneously, Plaintiffs argue they have standing because their injuries are “inextricably intertwined” with the injuries suffered by Google’s new AI competitors in the general search services market…
Neither theory persuades.”
The plaintiffs did not clearly define the publishing markets they said Google was trying to dominate or adequately show that Google was likely to monopolize them.
Judge Mehta explained:
“By failing to define plausible markets, Plaintiffs do not make out a necessary element of their attempted monopolization claims. Their monopoly leveraging claims fall for the same reason. Both claims also suffer from other basic pleading deficiencies.
Count V of Chegg’s Amended Complaint and Count VI of the PMC Plaintiffs’ Amended Complaint must be dismissed.”
The memorandum opinion goes into a fair amount of detail to explain the ruling:
“…Chegg’s claim would still fail because it has not plausibly pleaded that Google has a dangerous probability of monopolizing a market in which it participates.”
Of Penske’s claim he writes:
“What’s more, this market definition, even if the court were to accept it, renders implausible their attempted monopolization theory.
Indeed, the PMC Plaintiffs do not even attempt to quantify Google’s market share in online publishing or offer a single fact to support their allegation that Google has a dangerous probability of monopolizing the sweeping market they describe.”
After the federal claims were dismissed, the judge also dismissed the California unjust enrichment claims rather than decide them in federal court.
Judge Mehta explained:
“Having dismissed all federal claims in these actions, the court has discretion to either exercise supplemental jurisdiction over the remaining state law claims or decline to do so.
(dismissing all federal law claims and declining to exercise supplemental jurisdiction over remaining state law claims, including unjust enrichment). In exercising this discretion, the court considers “judicial economy, convenience, and fairness to litigants.
In the typical case in which all federal-law claims are dismissed, the “‘balance of factors to be considered . . . will point toward declining to exercise jurisdiction over the remaining state-law claims.'” “
Judge Mehta’s ruling will no doubt be disappointing to publishers and SEOs around the world who feel that Google is abusing its position of market dominance in search. A careful reading of the memorandum shows that the judge dismissed the complaints because the plaintiffs failed to allege facts that met the requirements of federal antitrust law.
The judge’s ruling was not saying the publishers suffered no harm. He was saying the complaints, as pleaded, did not satisfy the legal requirements for those antitrust claims.

HomeBlog 
Researchers at Northeastern University, in collaboration with Consumer Reports, evaluated how much modern cars spy in their drivers:
The new Northeastern study shows, for the first time, data flowing among the vehicles, the vehicle apps you download when you buy your car, and third-party companies, documenting exactly what kind of data gets siphoned from our vehicles and which companies are receiving that information. 
Basically, your car’s manufacturer has you under constant surveillance, and they use that data against you.
The companies on the receiving end of your data, our investigation has found, include car insurers and lenders that are partners in “telematics data exchanges,” which compile driving data on millions of drivers, thousands of data brokers that create personalized risk scores, companies selling infotainment and WiFi hotspot products, and even local and state government agencies working on planning, traffic, and safety initiatives.
Nearly every automaker sent data to outside companies.
Even more troubling, almost a quarter of the vehicle apps were found to be sending out personally identifiable information, including vehicle owners’ names, vehicle identification numbers (VINs), and precise geographic locations. That information can make it easy for companies to link driving behavior to personal data profiles created by data brokers and marketers. Such profiles are routinely sold to banks, insurers, pharmaceutical companies, lenders, and retailers, who can use it for personalized loan terms and filtered bank and insurance offers, a CR and CalMatters investigation found.
Remember the adage “If you’re not the customer, then you’re the product”? (The sentiment is older than you think.) Turns out that with modern internet-connected everything, you’re the product even if you are the customer.

I missed a crucial test-case from the university researchers’ report: what transmissions are being sent from the vehicle and the companion apps if they deny all consent?
The answer is not zero/nothing. Data is still collected even if the user doesn’t consent to anything.
I just wrote about this regarding the Volvo EX30:
https://github.com/muzso/volvocars_research/blob/main/volvo_ex30_vs_privacy.md

JonS •

					
						October 1, 2026 8:30 PM					

@Supreme
“It would be good to have both a privacy and security scorecard for car companies (and really any company providing services to customers).”
Indeed
However bear in mind that terms of service/terms of use for digital products tend to change on a whim.
Say you’ve paid US$60,000 for your shiny new Model Y because it has the best privacy and security scores available on the market.
Yeah, yeah, I know. Just go with it for the purposes of the example.
Anyway, you’re $60k in the hole. Then early next week they change their privacy rules a bit, so their privacy score gets a bit worse. What do you do? They they update the software again next month, and the privacy score gets a bit worse again. What do you do? Then over Thanksgiving Weekend they quietly push out yet another mandatory software update to improve braking performance which coincidentally lowers the privacy score still further – what do you do?
Maybe you are in the fortunate position where you can suck up the instant depreciation-on-purchase and ditch the car to replace it with something else which still has the level of privacy you demand. But a lot of people do not have that level of financial security, a group which probably includes the person who’s just bought your old, now un-private, Model Y.
Replace [Model Y] with [smartfridge], [TV], [etc.], …

49,2026-10-01,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
The relationships that matter most tend to find founders who build something worth noticing.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
Most entrepreneurs spend a lot of time thinking about networking, and it makes sense. Industry events, conferences, panels, coffee meetings and dinners can lead to customers, partners, hires and investors that would never come through a cold channel.
But I think many founders misunderstand what kind of networking actually pays off.
In my view, the best networking is a byproduct of doing excellent work, serving customers well and becoming known for something specific. When that happens, the right relationships tend to find you. Investors reach out because the company is working. Customers refer you because the experience was great. Other operators want to meet you because you’ve built something worth understanding. The strongest networks rarely come from handing out the most business cards. They come from trust earned again and again over time.
Networking can become a distraction, especially early on. It can look like spending time at panels and conferences hoping one meeting will fix the underlying challenge of building the business, while the fundamentals, such as the product, customer experience and follow-up, aren’t where they need to be.
The uncomfortable truth is that no amount of networking will make up for a weak product, inconsistent service or a lack of customer trust. If the product isn’t working, more introductions usually won’t fix it. If customers don’t feel taken care of, more surface-level relationships won’t create a lasting business.
I’ve been there myself. A coffee meeting can feel productive because it creates motion. You had a conversation, made a new contact and maybe got some advice. But motion isn’t progress, and it’s easy to confuse the two when the real work is harder to face.
For a founder, the real work might be talking to customers, improving the product, tightening the business model or making a hard hiring decision. These tasks may not feel as exciting as meeting new people, but they’re usually what make networking work later.
I don’t want to dismiss relationships altogether. I’ve benefited enormously from a core group of founder friends, and that’s very different from general networking.
These are people who know me, understand my business and have lived through enough of the founder journey that I can be honest with them in ways that are harder with employees, investors or board members. They understand the pressure of making decisions with incomplete information, being responsible for other people’s livelihoods and pushing forward when the answer isn’t obvious. Peers like that challenge your thinking and share what they’ve learned, which is incredibly valuable.
What makes these relationships work is depth, trust and shared context. They aren’t random contacts collected at events. They’re people who have seen how you work, understand what you’re building and have a reason to stay invested in your progress.
For some people, that network already exists through school, past companies, former colleagues or years in the same industry. Others have to build it more intentionally.
If you’re an early founder without a real network, I recommend spending time in a high-talent environment before starting your own company. Work at a great startup. Join a team with excellent engineers, operators, salespeople and product thinkers. Learn how strong people work, build trust with them and form relationships based on doing hard things together.
The same principle applies in any industry: the best relationships come from real collaboration and follow-through. Take real estate. The agents with the strongest referral networks usually aren’t the ones at every event. They’re the ones who make the lender’s job easier, communicate well with attorneys and vendors, and protect the client experience when a deal gets stressful. Over time, people remember who made the work smoother and who handled pressure well.
Targeted outreach still has value when it’s done well. If you reach out to someone a few years ahead of you, in a relevant market, with a specific reason for wanting their perspective, many people will respond. Most founders had help along the way, and there’s a natural instinct to pay it forward when a request feels genuine.
A thoughtful, specific question will usually get a better response than a vague request to “pick your brain.” Someone who studies how another founder built their business and asks about one relevant decision is far more likely to make a real connection than someone sending the same message to 50 people.
Follow-through is what turns that first conversation into something meaningful. When someone asks for advice, acts on it, reports back on what happened and keeps the other person updated, the relationship changes. That’s the kind of networking that works: specific, earned and grounded in action.
The best network is usually a byproduct of doing the work so well that people want to be close to it. Build something strong, serve people well, follow through and become known for a clear standard. The relationships that matter will follow.
Most entrepreneurs spend a lot of time thinking about networking, and it makes sense. Industry events, conferences, panels, coffee meetings and dinners can lead to customers, partners, hires and investors that would never come through a cold channel.
But I think many founders misunderstand what kind of networking actually pays off.
In my view, the best networking is a byproduct of doing excellent work, serving customers well and becoming known for something specific. When that happens, the right relationships tend to find you. Investors reach out because the company is working. Customers refer you because the experience was great. Other operators want to meet you because you’ve built something worth understanding. The strongest networks rarely come from handing out the most business cards. They come from trust earned again and again over time.

Apple's stock Camera app lets iPhone 18 Pro owners choose from a four-stop variable aperture ranging from ƒ/1.48 to ƒ/4.0, delivering around 50 percent better low-light performance and giving users more optical control over depth of field.
Halide 3.2 does away with that four-stop limitation and instead lets you select any ƒ-stop setting within the camera's full range. 
There are two styles of aperture control in the app. The first is Aperture Priority mode, where you can set the ƒ-stop and the app handles the shutter speed and ISO to properly expose the shot.

Halide 3.2 is available to download on the App Store, and costs $69.99 as a one-time purchase price, or $19.99 for an annual subscription.
