The artist’s globe-trotting exhibition touches down in North America
As Chiharu Shiota’s exhibit “The Soul Trembles” traverses the globe, she cuts down her immersive thread installations and restrings them at the next destination. Born in Osaka, Shiota has lived in Berlin since 1996 where she continues to conceive her stunning works in fine art and performance. Her dramatic creations capture the imagination of museum goers with record setting attendance around the world. With this opening in Montreal, curated by Mami Kataoka of the Mori Art Museum in Tokyo and Iris Amizlev of the Montreal Museum of Fine Arts (MMFA), the celebrated exhibition docks for the first time in North America.
“This is my life. All of my emotions are in my work,” Shiota says as the final touches were being put on the installation. After each exhibition she knows that the threads will be cut down and sculptural installations are reduced to piles of string. “I hope people can keep the memories and that their memories will last forever,” she says. 
“I’m very happy that the set-up is done,” says Shiota at an early preview of the exhibition. “Everyone was so welcoming, and the opening was such a wonderful event. Montreal is beautiful.” Though the process of installing a show as large as “The Soul Trembles” means managing a team of several local students and artists, Shiota was able to find a little time to explore Montreal’s outdoor markets including the colorful Marche Jean-Talon that originally opened in 1933. 
In a building designed by architects Edward and William S. Maxwell in 1912, the MMFA’s Beaux Arts white marble façade and a tall colonnaded portico may seem like a surprising setting for Shiota’s contemporary works. To enter the show, museum guests ascend the grand staircase under ghostly white boats suspended overhead, flanked in black fringe. This dramatic fleet is entitled “Where are We Going?” and offers a harbinger of the journey ahead. 
This is my life. All of my emotions are in my work.
Inside the first room, a sweet flower painting from Shiota’s childhood greets visitors. On the same wall, a vibrant abstract painting marks the time when she quit painting, citing an inability to fully express herself in the medium. What transpired next was a series of performance art explorations, where she used her body and other materials. In “Becoming Painting,” Shiota covered herself in red enamel paint. She later wrapped herself in fabric, surrounded in tubes that resembled the flow of blood. Her arrival in Berlin led to studying with Marina Abramovic and participating in many body-focused photo and video works, some of which are displayed as visual representation of her evolution as a young artist. 
The next gallery features an early fabric installation and a giant platform covered with doll furniture and flea market finds. These small objects have been painstakingly attached to each other with varying widths of red threads connecting her miniature world. In an art landscape that continues to push the boundaries of digital art, AI creations and technological advances, this focus on tactile bonds, from delicate to bold, throughout “The Soul Trembles” feels particularly poignant. Shiota can take viewers on an immersive, otherworldly journey inside her hand-woven thread creations. 
One gallery features imagery and video from her theatre, dance and opera stage designs. “My last theatre project was the stage design for “Idomeneo, re di Creta” in 2024,” says Shiota. “Working with choreographer Sidi Larbi Cherkaoui was a great experience. I enjoy creating for the stage. It’s very different from an exhibition because I have to work together with many people and the performers have to interact with all the materials.” 
“The Soul Trembles” continues with a large installation of windows called “Inside – Outside” that sits across from two hand-sewn white dresses with a mirror that are encased in a giant box frame covered in black threads. In the next room Shiota enrobes a hauntingly beautiful burned piano in black strings that wind their way out to an audience of chairs and every corner with arched passageways to navigate through.
In “Uncertain Journey,” the centerpiece of the show and the most recognizable imagery of her work, red threads burst from the frames of small boats swirling in tangles to the edges of the walls and ceiling. Looking at her installation work in photos and videos offer a glimpse of the visceral feeling of wandering through the beauty of Shiota’s world building in person.
The MMFA installation is the 10th iteration of “The Soul Trembles” since its original opening in 2019 at the Mori Art Museum in Tokyo. Shiota brings the elements to each stop on the tour, but needs to reshape accordingly to inhabit each museum’s unique architecture. “The artworks themselves do not change,” she explains. “But the political climate and the museum space are always different, so the feeling within the installation might change. Sometimes the museum’s own collection is presented right next to my work, like at the MAO in Italy. Other times the museum asks me to create additional work specifically for the show.” 
Working with Kataoka and Amizlev to shape how the exhibit would inhabit the original MMFA building has become part of the artistic process for Shiota. “The ceiling is quite low compared to other presentations,” says Shiota. “So the feeling changes. For the installation “Accumulation: Searching for the Destination,” the suitcases are normally suspended, but for this location that wasn’t possible.” In a gallery on a lower floor, Shiota decided to stack the suitcases all the way up to the ceiling. 
Previous stops have gathered record-breaking audiences. “I am happy to see so many different people visit the exhibition, from children to elderly people,” says Shiota. “My artwork is about something deep and touches on fundamental questions about death, which I had when I had cancer. But some works are made with very mundane objects like doll furniture; they are connected to my life, and this is a life everyone can connect to, playing with dolls or facing the meaning of death. My work is very universal.”
As “The Soul Trembles” travels around the world, each stop on the tour eventually has a closing date. “I’m not sad about the de-installation, or about cutting the thread because I often already have the next destination planned,” says Shiota. “I’m excited to show at the next stop, and I’m thinking only about the future. The exhibition lives on in the memory of the viewer and memory is everything.”
“The Soul Trembles” is now on view at the Montreal Museum of Fine Arts (MMFA)  through 14 February, 2027.
Chiharu Shiota’s Soul Trembles at the Montreal Museum of Fine Arts

A week ago, to the surprise of many, No. 21 Florida was a home favorite against No. 4 Ole Miss.
""Everybody was asking: How could Florida be favored? Well, now we see how,"" Caesars Sports head of football trading Joey Feazel said.
Indeed, Florida blasted Ole Miss 52-28. Subsequently, one week after the public betting masses wanted nothing to do with the Gators, they’re all over Florida in college football Week 5 odds, vs. Missouri.
Bookmakers and sharp bettors serve up their insights on that matchup and more in college football Week 5 betting.
This page may contain affiliate links to legal sports betting partners. If you sign up or place a wager, FOX Sports may be compensated. Read more about Sports Betting on FOX Sports.
Last year, Florida went 4-8 straight up (SU) and 5-7 against the spread (ATS). Gators coach Billy Napier was fired halfway through the season, and John Sumrall was hired on Nov. 30.
Oddsmakers expected somewhat of a turnaround, putting Florida’s season win total at 7.5. But it’s doubtful that anyone thought the Gators would be unbeaten heading into Week 5.
Florida is 4-0 SU/3-1 ATS and coming off that hugely impressive blowout of Ole Miss, as a 4-point home favorite.
The Gators then jumped from No. 21 to No. 8 in the AP poll. And Caesars Sports opened Florida as a 4.5-point road favorite vs. Missouri (3-1 SU/1-3 ATS) in college football Week 5 odds.
As of Wednesday night, Florida was up to -6. Feazel said the big win rightly swung the public’s opinion.
""You’ve got to see it to believe it,"" Feazel said. ""They weren’t on Florida last week vs. Ole Miss. Now, we’re seeing a lot of Florida money.""
So undoubtedly, Caesars will need Missouri — and preferably in an outright upset — for Saturday’s 3:30 p.m. ET kickoff.
Aaron Philo and Florida have made believers out of the public (Getty Images).  
Even with a Week 2 loss at Texas, Ohio State (3-1 SU and ATS) is the favorite in Caesars’ College Football Playoff championship odds. That’s also despite being ranked fifth in the AP poll.
The Buckeyes are hefty road favorites vs. No. 17 Iowa (4-0 SU/3-1 ATS) in a 3:30 p.m. ET Saturday start. Ohio State opened -14 and initially backed up to -13.5, but on Wednesday morning climbed to -14.5, where the number stood Wednesday night.
""To no one’s surprise, we’re taking more Ohio State money,"" Feazel said. ""The need is gonna be Iowa hanging within the number.""
The FOX Big Noon Kickoff heads to Minneapolis this week, where Minnesota (3-1 SU and ATS) hosts Michigan (3-1 SU/2-2 ATS).
The Wolverines are coming off their first loss — many might argue it’s their second — in a 20-19 home setback to Iowa as 4.5-point home favorites. Michigan gave up a touchdown on the game’s final play.
On the flip side, the Golden Gophers are coming off a big road upset, going to Washington as 12.5-point underdogs and leaving with a 27-24 victory.
Caesars opened Michigan -6.5, and the first move was toward Minnesota for this noon ET Saturday kickoff. The line dipped to Wolverines -5.5 on Sunday night and was still there as of Wednesday night.
""Michigan finally got the loss they were due,"" Feazel said. ""There’s not a lot of action at the moment on the side. This will get some action on Saturday, but I think it’ll be mostly two-way.
""But there is action on the Under.""
The total opened at 44.5, then got as low as 43 multiple times before nudging to 43.5 on Wednesday night.
FOX also has an interesting Friday night matchup between Penn State (3-1 SU/2-2 ATS) and Northwestern (2-1 SU/3-0 ATS), at 8 p.m. ET.
Last week, Northwestern put the fear of God into Indiana before losing 29-23 as a 20.5-point road underdog. And Penn State, ranked 13th at the time, shockingly blew a 17-0 lead and lost to Wisconsin 24-20 as a 9.5-point home favorite.
DraftKings Sports has the Nittany Lions as 2.5-point road favorites vs. the Wildcats, and opinions are split midweek, with 54% of spread bets on Northwestern/61% of spread money on Penn State.
The Trojans are big favorites in Week 5 despite last week's loss to Oregon (Getty Images). 
College football betting expert Paul Stone recognizes that USC (4-1 SU/1-4 ATS) can ill afford another loss in the hugely competitive Big Ten.
Last week, the Trojans were tied with Oregon at 27 early in the fourth quarter. But Southern Cal didn’t score again in a 41-27 setback as a 3.5-point home underdog.
This week, USC hosts Washington (3-1 SU/0-4 ATS) in a 7:30 p.m. ET Saturday kick. The Trojans are 9.5-point favorites, and Stone believes a double-digit win is in store.
Explaining his optimism, Stone noted that USC is averaging 39.8 points and 489.4 yards a game, among the top 10 nationally in both categories.
""USC hasn't necessarily cured its defensive shortcomings. But the Trojans' offense remains among the nation's best,"" Stone said. ""On the other hand, Washington has been punchless on offense this season. Quarterback Demond Williams has no real weapons to complement his skills.
""I look for USC to roll to an easier-than-expected victory and cover the point spread.""
Florida-Missouri isn’t the only big SEC showdown this week. No. 8 Alabama (4-0 SU/3-1 ATS) visits surprisingly unbeaten and 16th-ranked Mississippi State (4-0 SU and ATS), in a noon ET Saturday start.
The Bulldogs are coming off a riveting 31-24 win as 3.5-point favorites vs. Missouri. QB Kamario Taylor has also put himself into the Heisman Trophy odds conversation, now the +600 third choice, trailing only Ohio State wideout and favorite Jeremiah Smith (+260) and Miami QB Darian Mensah (+375).
Still, Caesars opened the Crimson Tide as 6-point road favorites, with the line toggling to -5.5 a couple of times early in the week before resettling at -6.
""So far, people are betting on Alabama and betting on the Over,"" Feazel said. ""But we’re also starting to see some Mississippi State moneyline trickle in.
""So we’re probably going to need Alabama to win and Mississippi State to cover. Although Mississippi State outright probably wouldn’t be bad, because there’ll be a lot of Alabama moneyline parlays."""
